At least now they could identify the decision rather than imagine a machine had made it.

In early summer, a physician named Iris Bell-Reyes delivered the first child conceived before the Exchange and born inside the archive.

Iris had been the dual-maternity child whose legal existence once required a slash in a post-office database. She now carried both family names on a medical credential no surviving government could verify.

The mother, Asha Verma, had crossed two shelters after losing contact with her partner. She refused to name the missing woman dead.

"Parent field?" the records clerk asked.

"Leave room," Iris said.

"For what?"

"For whoever survives to answer."

The birth took eleven hours. The ventilation failed during the ninth. Eve worked the manual bag while an electrician powered the infant warmer from the school battery. A Muslim mechanic boiled instruments. A former Crusader medic held Asha's hand because every trained nurse was treating a burn injury near the reactor.

The child arrived angry and underweight.

Nobody called the cry a symbol.

They checked his airway.

The ration council increased Asha's food and reduced every other adult portion by less than a spoonful. Three people objected. All three left extra food beside her bed that night.

Iris wrote the birth on paper.

Under parentage she entered Asha's name, the missing partner's name, and a blank line.

"The old forms never had enough fields either," she told Nadiya.

"Did the law improve?"

"The punctuation did."

When the ground finally softened, they buried Paul Reyes.

His body had waited inside the sealed service chamber through the cold. His sister read the prayer again. His husband supplied the marriage date again, though the first record remained correct. Residents carried Paul across soil darkened by ash and meltwater.

They had no stone.

Darius gave them a broken air-filter frame. The husband scratched Paul's name into the metal and placed it above the grave.

The burial used labor needed elsewhere and returned nothing measurable.

No one proposed canceling it.

By autumn, the archive had exchanged medical filters for seed, repaired tools for salt, and copies of reactor instructions for a crate of antibiotics whose expiration date could not be verified. The first trade agreement fit on one page. Half of it described contamination testing. The other half described what happened if either settlement failed to deliver.

No flag appeared.

The agreement named the two water sources instead.

The first year had not produced a nation.

It had produced habits from which one might later be built: posted reasons, review dates, room on forms, clean water, names over graves, and trade specific enough to survive mistrust.

Nadiya opened the atlas to the children's drawings from 1889.

The customs station.

The orchard.

The cemetery and apartment.

The route to an aunt across three jurisdictions.

She added the caravan map.

Outside, people cleared ash from the intake grilles. A doctor argued with an electrician over power allocation. Someone cooked fungal protein in a steel drum and improved the smell with spices carried through the Exchange in a coat pocket. Two children built a wall of snow-dark bricks, then knocked it down because they wanted a road.

Nadiya walked beyond the outer door at noon.

The cold entered through every seam in her clothing. The sky had no clean edge between cloud and soot. Far to the north, a column of smoke marked another settlement or the end of one.

She had spent her life preserving warnings.

The Stability Index had seen structural discontinuity and been shelved. Wetwire reports had been hidden to prevent panic. Accerisma damage had been accepted because the compound worked. Emergency systems had protected states until states became dependent on emergency. Every faction had kept enough evidence to prove its fear and discarded enough to avoid its own reflection.

Seeing the end had not prevented it.

That did not make seeing worthless.

It changed what the record was for.

The record could not save the dead. It could deny survivors the comfort of one clean cause. It could preserve the choices inside the catastrophe, including the choices that had not been made.

Behind her, the archive door opened.

The boy with the blue pencil stepped into the cold.

"Are we the last people?" he asked.

"No."

"How do you know?"

Nadiya looked toward the radio mast, the caravan tracks, and the black sky above a world whose maps had failed again.

"Because someone answered the morning."

The boy considered that, then took her gloved hand.

They went inside before the light faded.
